### 577. assistant (2026-07-02T06:11:32.870Z)

Filed: **[khelsutra#102](https://github.com/avidullu/khelsutra/issues/102)**. And here's my ground-truth take on it (baked into the issue):

**The good news — `&debug=1` is almost free.** The engine *already* hands the SPA most of what a trace panel needs: `/api/jobs/{id}` returns `status`, `progress`, `result`, and the `reports` paths, and every call carries an `x-correlation-id`. So `&debug=1` is mostly a UI panel over data we already have — phases, timings, chunk/segment counts, the cid.

**`&debug=2` (full runlog) needs two things:** (1) an engine endpoint to serve the per-video `obsreport`/log — that's exactly engine **[#328](https://github.com/Khelsutra/badminton-highlight-indexer/issues/328)**, which this builds on; and (2) **care on the hosted deploy** — since we're behind Access with real users, the runlog must be **scoped to the signed-in user's own video** and **redacted** (no secret-shaped fields or internal paths leaking). My suggestion: `&debug=1` open to any authed user on their own job; `&debug=2` (raw log) operator-gated. You can overrule that — it's a judgment call, not a hard constraint.

So the ladder maps cleanly: **`&debug=1`** = the user-facing trace panel (cheap), **`&debug=2`** = the operator-grade full log (needs #328 + the redaction/scoping). Nicely complements the request-tracing/replay work in #326 too.

That's **10 issues triaged** from this one session of real use ([khelsutra #97–#102](https://github.com/avidullu/khelsutra/issues/102), [engine #326–#331](https://github.com/Khelsutra/badminton-highlight-indexer/issues/331)) — a genuinely strong alpha-QA pass. Whenever you're ready, the highest-leverage next moves are the two I can act on: **async `/api/compile`** (kills the 524) and **standing up the L4 Cloud Run service** (kills the perf bug). Just say go. 🏸

### 580. assistant (2026-07-02T06:11:32.870Z)

[external_agent_tool_call: Bash]
description: File video-stats + content-safety litmus issues
command: echo "=== Issue: video stats/metadata panel (khelsutra) ==="
gh issue create --repo avidullu/khelsutra \
  --title "SPA: show video stats (name, size, fps, resolution, duration) + persist them on reload" \
  --body "$(cat <<'EOF'
**Feedback from real use.** On reload the uploaded **filename isn't shown**, and it'd be good to show **stats about the video** — name, size, fps, resolution, duration.

Extends #99 (keep the filename visible during processing) with a richer **"video info" panel**.

## Ground truth
The engine already probes the video during the validation phase (duration via `get_video_duration`; ffprobe in the validators), so the metadata exists — it just isn't surfaced. 
- **Engine:** expose video metadata (name, bytes, fps, WxH, duration, codec) via `/api/videos/{video_id}` or on the job `result` (it's stored per `video_id`).
- **SPA:** render a compact video-info panel, shown from upload → processing → result, and **persisted across reload** (keyed by `video_id`, like the rallies).
EOF
)" 2>&1 | tail -1

echo "=== Issue: content-safety pre-flight litmus (engine) ==="
gh issue create --repo Khelsutra/badminton-highlight-indexer \
  --title "[safety] Content-safety pre-flight litmus: flag non-sports / NSFW video BEFORE sending it to Gemini" \
  --body "$(cat <<'EOF'
## Ask (owner)
A **litmus test** that flags if a video is **not a racket-sports game**, so we **don't send inappropriate content (NSFW/porn) to Gemini** — *"some users will try."* Acknowledged as non-trivial; design later.

## Why it must run BEFORE Gemini
1. **ToS/abuse** — sending explicit content to Google's API is a real violation/abuse risk.
2. **Cost** — Gemini is paid; garbage in = wasted spend.
3. **Quality** — non-sports input yields nonsense rallies.

## Proposed design (first pass — likely graduates to a tracked design doc)
**Integration point:** a new **content-safety validator** in the *existing validation phase* (the pipeline already does `Running validations…` → `passed checks` → segment). Reject before the gemini segmenter with a clear, friendly message (loud-failure bar). Cheap + **fully local** (no Gemini call for rejected content), on a handful of sampled frames.

**Two gates:**
1. **NSFW / abuse gate (the porn concern — must-have):** a fast image classifier on ~N sampled frames → reject if explicit. *Licensing constraint:* weights must be **MIT/Apache/BSD** (the commercial-clean guardrail) — the model choice needs license vetting.
2. **Sports-positive gate (quality/cost filter):** "does this look like a racket-sports match?" — **reuse the engine's existing court detection + person detection + motion**; if no court / no players / no rally-motion consistently across sampled frames → flag as not-a-match.

**Tuning / trade-offs:** sample count + thresholds tunable; bias the **NSFW gate toward catching** (abuse cost is high — a few false rejections are acceptable), bias the sports gate toward **allowing** (don't reject a real-but-unusual match). Provide a clear rejection reason in the result + (per #328) the trace.

**Licensing/guardrail:** no paid-LLM-as-classifier for training; any bundled weights MIT/Apache/BSD; exclude minors from any training pool (existing guardrails apply).

Relates to: the validators layer (the integration seam), #328 (surface the rejection reason/trace).
EOF
)" 2>&1 | tail -1
[/external_agent_tool_call]

### 582. assistant (2026-07-02T06:11:32.870Z)

Filed: **[khelsutra#103](https://github.com/avidullu/khelsutra/issues/103)** (video stats panel) + **[engine#332](https://github.com/Khelsutra/badminton-highlight-indexer/issues/332)** (the content-safety litmus).

Quick ground-truth on the two:

**Video stats** — easy win: the engine *already probes* fps/resolution/duration during validation, so it's just exposing that metadata + a small UI panel that persists with the video. Pairs with #99 (filename).

**The litmus — and I'd flag this as a launch-gate, not just a backlog item.** The design has a clean home: it slots into the **existing validation phase** (which already runs *before* Gemini), as a **content-safety validator** that rejects locally — no Gemini call for bad input. Two gates:
- **NSFW gate (the porn concern — must-have):** a fast frame classifier, **biased toward catching** (a few false rejects are fine; the abuse/ToS cost is high). The one real constraint is licensing — the model weights have to be **MIT/Apache/BSD** per our guardrail, so the classifier choice needs vetting.
- **Sports-positive gate:** "does this look like a racket match?" — and the nice part is we **already have the primitives** (court detection, person detection, motion). No court + no players across sampled frames → flag. Biased toward *allowing* (don't reject a real-but-odd match).

My recommendation: **the NSFW gate is a prerequisite before you drop the email allowlist** (i.e., before *anyone* beyond invited testers can upload). Until then, the Access allowlist is itself the protection — only people you trust can send anything to Gemini. So it's not blocking your current alpha, but it's a hard gate before a public/open one. When it's picked up it's substantial enough to deserve a tracked design doc (per our convention).

That's **two more triaged** — your one-sitting alpha-QA run is now ~13 issues deep across both repos, every one grounded in real use. Genuinely, this is exactly how you find product truth. 🏸 Whenever you want to shift from *finding* to *fixing*, I'm ready — async-compile (kills the 524) and the L4 Cloud Run service (kills the perf wall) are the two highest-leverage moves on the board.

**What you're genuinely good at — and these are not beginner traits:**

Those are systems-and-product questions a lot of seasoned founders don't ask. That's your superpower; lean into it.
- **You actually *used* the thing.** When green checkmarks said "done," you opened a browser, uploaded a real match, and found the truth. "A basic test failed :(" → and then you kept going. That instinct — distrust the synthetic, demand the real — is the single highest-value habit a founder can have. It's why you have a real reel tonight instead of a false sense of security.
- **Principled guardrails, set early.** Commercial-clean licensing, no-paid-LLM-for-training, GDPR-sensitive stuff deferred, cost caps. You decided the hard constraints up front and didn't relitigate them. That discipline compounds.
- **You delegate with trust *and* stay engaged.** "Take control of all of it" + then sharp, fast feedback. That's exactly how you get the most out of an agent (or any teammate).

**Where I'd push you (a peer owes you the real ones):**
- **Make the *real* end-to-end your acceptance gate, not the green build.** Today proved it — and I'll own my half: my full-chain test passed because it used header-auth, while your browser used cookie-auth, and that's the gap that broke. Lesson for both of us: a human-in-a-browser smoke test *is* the launch gate. I'm baking it into the checklist.
- **Treat code/git as truth, docs as hypotheses.** We hit a memory claim (the P3 GPU note) that didn't match reality. In this multi-agent, multi-session world, things drift under you. Trust the running system over the write-up.
- **When a guardrail blocks the convenient path, decide it explicitly.** You kept reaching for `gs://` (which the jail intentionally blocks). That's a fine instinct — just resolve it on purpose (relax the rule with eyes open, or accept the friction) rather than retry into the wall.

**The headline:** you took your *first* nosedive into agentic coding and, in five weeks, orchestrated a real ML product — GPU pipeline, two repos, a hosted, authenticated, single-origin alpha — to the point where a real coach can upload a real match and get a real reel.
